L49: ## 1 Executive Summary
L50: ## 1.1. Product Introduction
L51: On March 31, 2020, Y-mAbs Therapeutics, Inc. (Y-mAbs) submitted original Biologics License Application (BLA) under Section 351(a) of the Public Health Service Act seeking approval of naxitamab-gqgk (Danyelza) for the treatment of patients with refractory or relapsed high-risk neuroblastoma in bone or bone marrow in combination with granulocyte-macrophage colony-stimulating factor (GM-CSF).
L52: Naxitamab-gqgk (naxitamab, hu3F8-IgG1; will be referred to as naxitamab for the remainder of this document) is a humanized IgG1 monoclonal antibody directed against the glycolipid disialoganglioside (GD2), which is a member of the b-series gangliosides and an adhesion molecule widely expressed on the cell membrane in neuroectoderm-derived tumors; the established pharmacological class is GD2-binding monoclonal antibody. In vitro, naxitamab was shown to bind to cell surface GD2 and induce complement-dependent cytotoxicity (CDC) and antibody dependent cell-mediated cytotoxicity (ADCC). Naxitamab contains human framework regions and murine complementarity-determining regions. Naxitamab is produced from a Chinese hamster ovary cell line and has an approximate molecular weight of 144 kDa without glycosylation. Naxitamab injection is supplied as a sterile, preservative-free, clear to slightly opalescent and colorless to slightly yellow solution for intravenous infusion. Each single-dose vial contains 40 mg of naxitamab in 10 mL of solution. Each mL of solution contains 4 mg of naxitamab and citric acid anhydrous (0.71 mg), poloxamer 188 (1.5 mg), sodium chloride (7.01 mg), sodium citrate (6.3 mg), and Water for Injection, USP. The pH is approximately 5.7. Although the two trials supporting efficacy and safety in this application used different manufacturing processes, OBP determined that the products used in each trial were generally comparable. Naxitamab is a new molecular entity and has not been previously marketed in the U.S. or approved for any indication by FDA or globally.
L53: ## 1.2. Conclusions on the Substantial Evidence of Effectiveness
L54: In the opinion of the review team, the submitted evidence meets the statutory evidentiary standard for accelerated approval. The recommendation for accelerated approval is based on the results from two single-arm, open-label trials: Trial 201 (NCT 03363373) and Trial 12-230 (NCT 01757626).
L55: Both trials enrolled patients with relapsed or refractory high risk neuroblastoma in the bone or bone marrow who had demonstrated a response of minor response, partial response, or stable disease to prior therapy. Patients received naxitamab 9 mg/kg/cycle administered as three
L56: {DANYELZA, naxitamab-gqgk}
L57: separate intravenous infusions of 3 mg/kg (on Days 1, 3 and 5) in the first week of each cycle. Patients received GM-CSF subcutaneously at 250 µg/m2/day on Days -4 to 0 and at 500 µg/m2/day on Days 1 to 5. The primary efficacy population included patients with relapsed or refractory disease in the bone or bone marrow who had demonstrated a response of stable disease or better in response to prior therapy, had evaluable disease per blinded independent central review according to the revised International Neuroblastoma Response Criteria (INRC), and who received at least one dose of naxitamab. The primary efficacy population was comprised of 22 patients in Trial 201 and 39 patients in Trial 12-230.
L58: The analysis of efficacy demonstrated a clinically meaningful overall response rate (ORR) per independent review with sufficient duration to represent a clinically meaningful treatment effect in pediatric patients with relapsed and refractory neuroblastoma with disease in the bone or bone marrow. Patients in both trials were permitted to receive radiation in certain circumstances, although assessment of response excluded sites that received radiation. Patients for whom radiation was administered to the only site of evaluable disease, which applied only to patients in Trial 12-230, were excluded from the efficacy analysis. Patients with a best response of progressive disease to initial therapy or therapy at relapse were excluded from Trial 201 and from the efficacy analysis population in Trial 12-230. Therefore, the indication the review team proposes for approval also excludes these patients.
L59: In Trial 201 (n=22), the confirmed ORR in patients with metastatic, relapsed or refractory high-risk neuroblastoma in the bone or bone marrow who demonstrated a response of stable disease, partial response, or minor response to prior therapy per blinded, independent review was 45% (95% CI: 24%, 68%), with 30% of the 10 responders having observed duration of response (DOR) of at least 6 months. The primary efficacy analysis in Trial 201 included 4 patients with prior anti-GD2 treatment; one (25%) of these patients demonstrated a response.
L60: In Trial 12-230 (n=38), the confirmed ORR per blinded, independent review according to the revised INRC was 34% (95% CI: 20%, 51%), with 23% of the 13 responders having an observed DOR of 6 months. The primary efficacy analysis in Trial 12-230 included 22 patients with prior anti-GD2 treatment; 4 (18%) of these patients demonstrated a response (95% CI 5%, 40%). Given the limited number of patients with prior anti-GD2 exposure, the exploratory analyses of ORR in this subpopulation should be interpreted with caution; however, the results suggest that naxitamab has clinical activity in patients who received prior anti-GD2 antibody therapy. The difference in the point estimates of ORR between the two trials may be explained by the higher percentage of relapsed patients, who are more heavily pre-treated, in Trial 12-230 (55% vs. 36% in Trial 201).
L61: {DANYELZA, naxitamab-gqgk}
L62: Assessment of DOR was limited by administration of additional anticancer therapies subsequent to achieving a complete or partial response in patients in both trials. Neither study was designed to include scheduled assessments of response after completion of treatment with naxitamab, and Trial 201 is currently ongoing. Additional clinical data from Trial 201 may provide additional data to inform durability of responses.
L63: Given the rarity of relapsed or refractory high-risk neuroblastoma and the lack of an approved or standard treatment, a randomized trial is not likely to be feasible in this population. The review team considers that the observed ORR in the two trials, along with the DOR, in patients treated with naxitamab in combination with GM-CSF sufficient to provide substantial evidence of the effectiveness of naxitamab in the rare population of patients with relapsed or refractory neuroblastoma who have disease in the bone or bone marrow. In vitro data demonstrating ADCC with the monoclonal antibody mu3F8 (a previously studied, murine form of naxitamab) was provided to support the contribution of effect of GM-CSF. Dose-dependent ADCC was demonstrated with m3F8, while adding GM-CSF resulted in a reduction in concentration of mu3F8 required to mediate ADCC. Data from additional patients in this patient population, including ORR and DOR obtained from a broad representation of clinical sites, may be required to confirm clinical benefit.
L64: ## 1.3. **Benefit-Risk Assessment (BRA)**
L65: ## Benefit-Risk Summary and Assessment
L66: **Efficacy:** The effectiveness of naxitamab for the treatment of relapsed or refractory high-risk neuroblastoma with bone or bone marrow involvement was established based on data from 38 patients in Trial-230 and 22 patients in Trial 201. Both trials enrolled patients 1 year of age and older with primary refractory disease or incomplete response to salvage treatment in the bone or bone marrow. In Trial 201, the confirmed ORR per blinded, independent review was 45% (95% CI: 24%, 68%), with 30% of the 10 responders having an observed DOR of at least 6 months. The primary efficacy analysis in Trial 201 included 4 patients with prior anti-GD2 treatment, and 1 (25%) of these patients demonstrated a response. In Trial 12-230, the confirmed ORR per blinded, independent review according to the revised International Neuroblastoma Response Criteria (INRC) was 34% (95% CI: 20%, 51%), with 23% of the 13 responders having an observed DOR of 6 months. The primary efficacy analysis in Trial 12-230 included 22 patients with prior anti-GD2 treatment, and 4 (18%) of these patients demonstrated a response (95% CI 5%, 40%). Given the limited number of patients with prior anti-GD2 exposure, the exploratory analyses of ORR in this subpopulation should be interpreted with caution; however, the results suggest that naxitamab has some clinical activity in patients with prior anti-GD2 antibody exposure. The difference in the point estimates of ORR between the two trials may be explained by the higher percentage of relapsed patients, who are more heavily pre-treated, in Trial 12-230.
L67: Assessment of DOR was limited by administration of additional anticancer therapies subsequent to achieving a complete or partial response in patients in both trials. Neither study was designed to include scheduled assessments of response after completion of treatment with naxitamab, and Trial 201 is currently ongoing. Additional clinical data from Trial 201 may provide additional data to inform durability of responses.
L68: Although the investigational naxitamab used in the two trials supporting efficacy and safety in this application used different manufacturing processes, the Office of Biotechnology Products (OBP) determined that the products used in each trial were generally comparable, although
L69: direct comparability of potency was not possible given differences in potency assays used for each product (refer to page 32 of the OBP assessment for details).
L70: **Safety:** The safety of naxitamab in combination with GM-CSF was evaluated in a subpopulation of patients with relapsed or refractory neuroblastoma high-risk neuroblastoma in bone or bone marrow, from two open-label, single arm studies, Study 201 (n=25) and Study 12-230 (n=72). In both studies, patients received naxitamab as three separate intravenous infusions of 3 mg/kg (Days 1,3 and 5) in the first week of a 4-week cycle. Patients also received GM-CSF subcutaneously at 250 µg/m2/day on Days -4 to 0 and at 500 µg/m2/day on Days 1 to 5. No deaths due to an adverse event were reported. Cumulative safety data indicated that serious adverse reactions occurred in 32% of patients in Trial 201 and 40% of patients in Trial 12-230. Serious adverse reactions in occurring in &gt; 5% of patients in Trial 201 included anaphylactic reaction (12%) and pain (8%); in Trial 12-230 serious adverse reactions occurring in &gt;5% of patients included hypertension (14%), hypotension (11%), and pyrexia (8%).
L71: Naxitamab can cause serious infusion-related reactions and neurologic toxicity, including severe pain, transverse myelitis and reversible posterior encephalopathy syndrome (RPLS). Given the potential severity of these adverse reactions and the occurrence of infusion related reactions and pain in most patients in both trials, a boxed warning was included in Danyelza product labelling for both infusion reactions and neurotoxicity. FDA's pooled term analysis of infusion-related reactions included the following terms: hypotension, bronchospasm, flushing, wheezing, stridor, urticaria, dyspnea, pyrexia, infusion related reaction, face edema, swelling face, orbital edema, periorbital edema, lip edema, lip swelling, mouth edema, tongue edema, swollen tongue, mouth swelling, angioedema, respiratory tract edema, chills, hypoxia, pruritis, rash macular, rash maculo-papular, rash erythematous, rash, and respiratory distress. The analysis was restricted to events which occurred the day of the infusion of naxitamab or the day following an infusion.
L72: Additionally, hypertension is described in the Warnings and Precautions section of product labeling. The most common adverse reactions (≥25%) to naxitamab were infusion-related reaction, pain, tachycardia, vomiting, cough, nausea, diarrhea, decreased appetite, hypertension, fatigue, erythema multiforme, peripheral neuropathy, urticaria, pyrexia, headache, injection site reaction, edema, anxiety, localized edema and irritability. The most common Grade 3 or 4 laboratory abnormalities (≥5% in either study) were decreased lymphocytes, decreased neutrophils, decreased hemoglobin, decreased platelet count, decreased potassium, increased alanine aminotransferase, decreased glucose, decreased calcium, decreased albumin, decreased sodium and decreased phosphate.
L73: Clinically relevant adverse reactions in \le 10\% of patients who received naxitamab with GM-CSF in either study included apnea, hypopnea, generalized edema, peripheral edema, device related infection, anxiety, unresponsiveness to stimuli, and somnolence. Events of apnea, hypopnea, unresponsiveness and somnolence may be related to concomitant administration of opioids or to co-occurring events of severe infusion reactions, such as severe hypotension.
L74: The safety population included patients 1 to 24 years of age across both studies, with a median age of 5 years in both studies. Although relatively few young adult patients and very young patients (2 years of age or younger) were enrolled, exploratory analyses of safety trends did not demonstrate a significant difference in safety across groups.
L75: **Overall benefit:risk assessment:** Given the rarity of relapsed or refractory high-risk neuroblastoma and the lack of an approved or standard treatment, a randomized trial of naxitamab is not likely to be feasible in this population. The review team considers that the ORR in the two trials, along with the observed duration of responses, in patients treated with naxitamab is sufficient to establish clinical benefit in the rare group of patients with high risk neuroblastoma. Data from additional patients in this patient population including ORR and duration of response, may be required to confirm clinical benefit. The risks associated with naxitamab appear acceptable in the setting of patients with relapsed or refractory neuroblastoma who have no approved therapies. Naxitamab is expected to be administered in infusion centers with intensive monitoring and the ability to quickly respond to events such as severe infusion reactions. In addition, the existence of previously approved products in the US (dinutuximab) with a similar mechanism of action and toxicity profile means that the majority of pediatric oncologists administering naxitamab will have familiarity with expected toxicities associated with anti-GD2 antibodies. The review team considered the available safety and pharmacokinetic data, as well as the unmet medical need across age groups, in determining the age range (pediatric patients 1 year and older, as well as adult patients) included in the indication statement.
L76: In the opinion of the review team, naxitamab, in combination with GM-CSF, has a favorable benefit-risk profile for the treatment of pediatric patients 1 year of age and older and adult patients with relapsed or refractory high-risk neuroblastoma in the bone or bone marrow who have demonstrated a partial response, minor response, or stable disease to prior therapy.
L77: Dimension	Evidence and Uncertainties	Conclusions and Reasons
Analysis of Condition	Neuroblastoma is the most common extracranial tumor in children, with approximately 800 cases diagnosed each year in the United States (American Cancer Society website, 2020).
More than 50% of these patients present with distant metastasis (Stage 4, high-risk neuroblastoma) at the time of diagnosis, typically in the bone and bone marrow (Matthay, 1997).
Ten to 50% of patients with high-risk neuroblastoma do not achieve a remission after induction/consolidation therapy and are classified as refractory; 50% to 60% of those who complete the treatment will relapse (Cole, 2012; Marris, 2007; Cotterill, 2000).
The two-year post-progression/relapse survival rates range from 8% to 29%, and 10-year overall survival (OS) rates are approximately 14% (Cotterill, 2000).
Relapsed or refractory high-risk neuroblastoma in the bone or bone marrow is a serious and life threatening disease with a high unmet medical need.
Current Treatment Options	There are no FDA approved treatments for relapsed or refractory neuroblastoma with bone or bone marrow involvement (PDQ, 2020).
Patients are encouraged to enroll on clinical trials, are treated with off-label treatment or with palliative care.
Off label treatments include the following options (these data has not been reviewed by FDA):Chemotherapy with immunotherapy (temozolomide/irinotecan/dinutuximab); reported ORR 53% in a 25 patient

All currently available treatment options are palliative and are associated with significant toxicity.
Safe and effective treatments for this highly morbid condition are needed.

The confirmed overall response rate (ORR) in Trial 201 per blinded independent review was 45% (95% CI: 24%, 68%), with 30% of the 10 responders having observed DOR of at least 6 months.

L91: ## 2 Therapeutic Context
L92: ## 2.1. Analysis of Condition
L93: ## The Applicant's Position:
L94: Neuroblastoma is a rare, almost exclusively pediatric cancer. Neuroblastoma is the most common extracranial tumor in children, with about 800 cases diagnosed each year in the United States[1] The majority of patients are diagnosed before the age of 2 years (60%), and almost all are diagnosed before the age of 10 years (97%).[2] More than 50% of these patients present with distant metastasis (Stage 4) at the time of diagnosis, typically in the bone and bone marrow.[3-5]
L95: Clinical course and patient outcome are linked to the patient's risk categorization at the time of diagnosis. Risk classification is by the International Neuroblastoma Risk Group (INRG) Classification System.[6] Patients diagnosed with low-risk neuroblastoma (Stages 1, 2, and 4S, per the International Neuroblastoma Response Criteria [INRC]) or intermediate-risk neuroblastoma (Stage 3) have high survival rates, exceeding 95%. However, less than 50% of patients with high-risk disease achieve long-term survival.[6]
L96: Among patients with high-risk neuroblastoma, 10% to 50% do not achieve a remission after induction/consolidation therapy and are classified as refractory[3, 4, 7-9] and 50% to 60% of those who complete the treatment will relapse.[3, 4]
L97: For patients with high-risk neuroblastoma who relapse after induction/consolidation therapy, the sites of relapse are most commonly bone and bone marrow (BM). BM invasion is associated with a poor prognosis.[5, 10] The time to disease progression is another important predictor of outcome; in a follow-up analysis of relapsed patients, London et al. reported that patients at the highest risk of death were those who relapsed between 6 and 18 months after initial diagnosis (peak, approximately 12 months).[11]
L98: No standard of care currently exists for the treatment of patients with refractory or relapsed neuroblastoma, and the outcomes have been disappointingly poor for decades; the two-year post-progression/relapse survival rates range from 8% to 29%, and 10-year overall survival (OS) rates are around 14%.[5, 12]
L99: NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {BLA 761171}
L100: {DANYELZA, naxitamab-gqgk}
L101: Hence, there is a large, unmet medical need for more effective treatments for patients with refractory or relapsed neuroblastoma with disease in bone and/or bone marrow.
L102: ## The FDA's Assessment:
L103: FDA agrees with the description provided by the Applicant.
L104: {DANYELZA, naxitamab-gqgk}
L105: ## 2.2. Analysis of Current Treatment Options
L106: ## The Applicant's Position:
L107: Standard induction therapies for high-risk neuroblastoma include surgery and five to six cycles of induction chemotherapy with agents such as cisplatin, cyclophosphamide, doxorubicin, etoposide, topotecan, or vincristine. Consolidation therapy includes myeloablative chemotherapy regimens (e.g., carboplatin-etoposide-melphalan, busulfan melphalan, or thioteper-cyclophosphamide) with stem cell transplantation (SCT) and external-beam radiotherapy. Postconsolidation therapy to treat minimal residual disease (MRD) includes immunotherapy (e.g., dinutuximab) and cytokines (GM-CSF, interleukin-2), plus isotretinoin.[6, 13]
L108: A patient who does not respond fully to induction therapy or who relapses during or after consolidation/postconsolidation therapy can receive salvage treatment. Widely used salvage chemotherapy regimens combine topoisomerase I inhibitors (e.g., topotecan or irinotecan) with either alkylators (e.g., cyclophosphamide or temozolomide) or topoisomerase II inhibitors (e.g., doxorubicin or etoposide). If the topoisomerase I inhibitor-based regimens are ineffective, other chemotherapy salvage regimens available include ifosfamide, carboplatin, and etoposide. Patients with refractory or relapsed neuroblastoma have poor prognoses after incomplete response to or relapse after frontline therapy, respectively.[3] Particularly, there is an unmet medical need for effective treatment of refractory and relapsed patients with disease in bone and/or bone marrow after chemotherapy, who have a high probability of developing progressive disease with no approved treatment options.
L109: Because there are no approved treatment options for these patients, clinical trials are ongoing with investigational therapies for the treatment of relapsed or refractory neuroblastoma. Investigational therapies include CAR T-cell therapy, vaccines, antibody therapies (including anti-GD2 antibodies and checkpoint inhibitors), tyrosine kinase inhibitors, histone deacetylase inhibitors, and ornithine decarboxylase inhibitors.
L110: Hence, the treatment of relapsed and refractory neuroblastoma remains an area of unmet medical need.
L111: ## The FDA's Assessment:
L112: FDA agrees that there are no approved therapeutic options for patients with high-risk neuroblastoma that is relapsed or refractory. Treatment options commonly used at the time of recurrence (which are not approved for this indication) may include chemotherapy as described above, 131I-MIBG alone or in combination with other therapy, or chemotherapy in combination with anti-GD2 therapies (PDQ Pediatric Treatment Editorial Board, 2020).

L119: ## 3.2. Summary of Presubmission/Submission Regulatory Activity
L120: ## **The Applicant's Position:**
L121: Y-mAbs submitted IND 132793 on 05 September 2017 after acquiring the rights to naxitamab from Memorial Sloan Kettering Cancer Center (MSK). MSK had studied naxitamab under IND 112594 since 2012 and under IND [REDACTED](b) (4) As part of the pre-BLA meeting, FDA agreed with the use of MSK Trial 12-230 as the pivotal efficacy trial, with supportive efficacy data coming from Y-mAbs Trial 201 and retrospective Study 2PR01. MSK Trials 11-009 and 12-116 provide supportive safety data.
L122: After interactions with FDA, key changes to the naxitamab program included, but were not limited to: (1) incorporation of independent radiology and pathology reviews into response assessments for Trial 12-230 and Trial 201, (2) completion of comparability studies between the MSK-produced naxitamab and the Y-mAbs-produced naxitamab, (3) qualification of the nude rat model as a relevant animal model for naxitamab toxicology studies, (4) inclusion of multiple potency assays in the drug product release specifications, (5) pooling of data from Trial 11-009, Trial 12-116, Trial 12-230, and Trial 201 in a population PK study, and (6) conduct of a retrospective review of the naxitamab compassionate-use program in Spain for additional efficacy and safety data.

Trial 12-230, a primary study supporting efficacy and safety for the BLA, was conducted under the MSKCC IND.

Y-mAbs was permitted to use only a single lot in their Study 201 trial and no other lots could be used until Y-mAbs proposed a strategy to improve the performance of the ADCC-CD16, ADCC-CD32, and
L130: CDC assays and establish a meaningful acceptance criterion for each assay. The partial hold was removed on June 7, 2018 after Y-mAbs provided a complete response to partial clinical hold addressing these deficiencies.
L131: Agreements at the November 6, 2018 Type B meeting are summarized below:
L132: - FDA agreed that Y-mAbs's justification for the GM-CSF dosing regimen in combination with naxitamab in patients aged 12 months and older who have relapsed or refractory neuroblastoma high-risk neuroblastoma is acceptable and stated that a final determination of the adequacy of the dosing regimen will be made at the time of review of the planned BLA submission.
- FDA stated that in the context of a single-arm, single center study, a blinded independent review of all imaging and pathologic response data by expert pediatric radiologists and pathologists outside of MSKCC will reduce the risk for reader bias and be the most reliable process for the efficacy determination.
- FDA agreed that demonstration of an effect on centrally reviewed ORR of sufficient magnitude and duration with an acceptable safety profile for naxitamab in the intended patient population might support the accelerated approval of naxitamab, in combination with GM-CSF, for the treatment of high-risk neuroblastoma refractory to initial therapy or with incomplete response to salvage therapy in patients &gt;12 months of age with (b) (4), refractory disease in bone marrow, (b) (4) or bone (b) (4) FDA stated that the final indication will depend on review of the efficacy data for all patients and relevant subgroups.

FDA requested that any early efficacy data from the expanded access trial or Y-mAbs Study 201 also be submitted in the BLA, given that Study 12-230 is limited to a single institution's experience, and given that the product administered to patients in the trials was different.

L140: ## 4 Significant Issues from Other Review Disciplines Pertinent to Clinical Conclusions on Efficacy and Safety
L141: ## 4.1. Office of Scientific Investigations (OSI)
L142: One clinical site, Memorial Sloan Kettering Cancer Center (MSKCC) was selected for inspections because all patients enrolled in Trial 12-230 and 8 of 25 patients enrolled in the safety population of Trial 201 were treated at this site. The only other site at which more than one patient in the efficacy population for Trial 201 was treated was Hospital Sant Joan de Deu, located in Spain, which enrolled 16 of 25 patients in the safety population. Given the challenges associated with international travel during the COVID-19 pandemic and the ability to inspect the MSKCC site (Principal Investigator Dr. Brian Kushner) which generated the majority of the clinical data across studies, inspection of the clinical site in Spain was not deemed critical to the application review.
L143: Review of inspections of the imaging facility used for independent central assessment of ORR and DOR, [REDACTED](b) (4), were last completed in July 2018 and had been inspected three times since 2012. Each inspection had a compliance classification of No Action Indicated. Therefore, inspection of the imaging facility was not deemed necessary for this application.
L144: The inspection of the MSKCC clinical site conducted by the Office of Scientific Investigations revealed no significant findings. The studies appear to have been conducted adequately and there was no evidence of underreporting of serious adverse events or significant protocol deviations. Based on the inspection, the data generated by the inspected clinical investigator appear to be acceptable in support of the BLA.

L159: ## 5 Nonclinical Pharmacology/Toxicology
L160: ## 5.1. Executive Summary
L161: ## The FDA's Assessment:
L162: Naxitamab (hu3F8-IgG1) is a humanized monoclonal antibody against the ganglioside GD2, which is a member of the b-series gangliosides and an adhesion molecule widely expressed on the cell membrane in neuroectoderm-derived tumors; the established pharmacological class is GD2-binding monoclonal antibody. An earlier version of the antibody studied clinically, a mouse anti-GD2 monoclonal antibody coded m3F8, had promising clinical outcomes in patients with neuroblastoma; however, m3F8 also had significant immunogenicity in patients, leading the original investigators to develop the humanized version of m3F8, naxitamab. Naxitamab has the same epitope-targeting region of the murine antibody. In binding studies, naxitamab bound to human GD2 with a K_D of 19.8 nM. Naxitamab had no off-target binding to other gangliosides including GM2, GD1a, GT1b, GD3, and GQ1b, and had minimal binding to ganglioside GD1b (4.5% compared to maximal binding to GD2).
L163: In the absence of human serum or effector cells, naxitamab had direct cytotoxicity against the GD2 expressing human neuroblastoma cell line LAN-1 with EC_{50} values of 5 \mug/mL (approximately 29 times lower than the C_{max} of 147 \mug/mL in patients treated with naxitamab for multiple cycles on Days 1, 3, and 5 at 9.0 mg/kg). In antibody dependent cellular cytotoxicity (ADCC) assays, including assays using human peripheral blood mononuclear cells (PBMCs), polymorphonuclear leukocytes (PMNs), or CD16 or CD32 expressing NK cell lines as effector cells, naxitamab showed significantly increased activity against high GD2-expressing LAN-1 neuroblastoma target cells and 5 other GD2-expressing cell lines compared to the original murine version of the antibody. Conversely, naxitamab had mild decreases in CDC-mediated cytotoxicity compared to the murine parent antibody. No tested anti-GD2 antibody mediated significant ADCC or CDC activity against a low GD2-expressing cell line. Investigators also showed that the addition of granulocyte-macrophage colony stimulating factor (GM-CSF) to PBMC cultures resulted in concentration-dependent increases in m3F8-mediated ADCC against human neuroblastoma cell lines of up to 30% compared to m3F8 alone, providing support for the use of GM-CSF in the clinical dosing regimen for naxitamab.
L164: In an in vivo study, twice-weekly IV naxitamab administration resulted in dose-dependent anti-tumor activity in mice bearing LAN-1 subcutaneous tumors and decreased tumor size to a greater extent than m3F8 given on the same schedule. Further, Bergman and colleagues (1993) showed that m3F8 alleviated paraparesis caused by epidurally-implanted metastatic
L165: {DANYELZA, naxitamab-gqgk}
L166: neuroblastoma in a rat model due to m3F8-induced shrinkage of tumors in the epidural space. The Applicant submitted additional in vivo data from publications using a surrogate anti-GD2 antibody targeting a separate GD2 epitope to further explore the known naxitamab-related clinical side effect of pain. Intravenous or intrathecal administration of a murine IgG2a isotype anti-GD2 monoclonal antibody (14.G2a) to adult immunocompetent Sprague Dawley rats led to mechanical allodynia, confirming the enhanced pain effect of anti-GD2 administration in animals.
L167: The initial clinical trials investigating the activity and dosing of naxitamab were conducted on the basis of previous clinical data with the murine parent antibody rather than supported by toxicology data with the clinical candidate. As clinical development proceeded, FDA advised the manufacturer that the Agency would expect additional animal toxicology data to support a future BLA submission. The investigators asserted that nude rats rather than a non-human primate would be a reasonable toxicology model and confirmed the presence of NK cells and PMN in these animals prior to conducting toxicology studies. Investigators reasoned that the use of nude rats rather than fully immune competent animals would allow for avoidance of the formation of anti-drug antibodies that would prevent long-term assessment of a human antibody in a rodent model. To help address FDA concerns regarding the use of immunodeficient rats as pharmacologically relevant species for toxicological testing of naxitamab, Y-mAbs also evaluated the ability of naxitamab to mediate ADCC using effector cells from nude rats against rat GD2-expressing target cells and compared these effects to naxitamab-induced ADCC using human effector cells against human GD2-expressing target cells. Naxitamab did bind to both rat GD2 and to splenocytes from nude rats; however, in the presence of nude rat splenocyte effector cells, naxitamab did not induce ADCC against GD2-expressing rat PC12 cells and induced only ~10% cytotoxicity in GD2-expressing rat C6 cells. Naxitamab induced approximately 22% ADCC in human LAN-1/rat splenocyte co-cultures whereas there was 55% ADCC against the same cell line in the presence of human NK cells, indicating that the rat/rat and rat/human systems underpredicted the activity of the human/human system. Naxitamab also led to 24 to 54% ADCC at 30 and 100 µg/mL, respectively, against another GD2-expressing human neuroblastoma cell line, IMR-32. Additional studies to support selection of the nude rat for use in pivotal toxicity studies included evaluation of the reactivity of naxitamab in tissue cross-reactivity studies using peripheral nerve, skin, spinal cord, and brain sections from human, Sprague-Dawley rat, and nude rats. Staining with naxitamab occurred in the cerebral cortex, cerebellum, spinal cord, and peripheral nerve in tissues, and skin from human and both rat strains. Significantly stronger positive staining occurred in human tissues compared to both rat strains.
L168: {DANYELZA, naxitamab-gqgk}
L169: Y-mAbs conducted a GLP-compliant 13-week toxicology study in nude rats. Rats received intravenous naxitamab for 4 cycles of 3 treatment days per cycle (every other day on Days 1, 3, and 5) at doses of 0, 10, or 50 mg/kg. Exposures achieved in rats at the 50 mg/kg dose level were 32 (males) and 25 (females) times higher than the AUC in patients (2386 \mu\text{g} \cdot \text{h}/\text{mL}) at the once every 3 days per cycle clinical dose of 9 mg/kg. Multiple deaths occurred in all dosing groups including the control group, with mortality rates of 13% in controls, 20% in the 10 mg/kg group, and 13% in the 50 mg/kg group; the causes of death were undetermined and without notable patterns of clinical signs or pathology, suggesting that they were not related to the test-article. In surviving animals, naxitamab-related toxicity was generally mild. Naxitamab caused decreased body weight gain of up to 12% in both the 10 and 50 mg/kg groups compared to control animals which persisted into the recovery phase. Rats treated with both doses of naxitamab showed minimal increases in reticulocytes, neutrophils, lymphocytes, eosinophils, basophils, platelets, and APTT with no dependence on dose level, and decreased prothrombin in the 50 mg/kg group as well as minimal increases in urea and potassium. All clinical chemistry and hematology findings recovered by the end of the recovery period. Minor decreases in organ weights of the thyroid/parathyroid occurred at both dose levels. Gross pathology findings were limited to the stomach with depression and thickening noted at both dose levels which was further supported by histopathology findings of hyperplasia, inflammation, and erosion of the stomach. Additional histopathology findings included minimal to moderate inflammatory cell infiltrate/inflammation of the plantar and tibial nerves in treated males. Evaluation of nociception (pain response) using an analgesiometer and application of increasing pressure on the paw between a flat surface and a blunt pointer showed a mild increase in pain threshold, which is in contrast to the expected pharmacological response due to treatment with an anti-GD2 antibody, and in contrast to the responses shown by Sprague Dawley rats treated with a surrogate anti-GD2 antibody as reviewed in the pharmacology section as well as clinical findings of increased pain sensitivity. The lack of increased pain response in adult nude rats and a similar lack of a clear effect in a non-GLP dose range finding study in juvenile nude rats coupled with the in vitro ADCC data using nude rat effector and target cells showing significantly reduced activity compared to the human counterparts suggests that the immunodeficient nude rat does not fully recapitulate the activity of drug and therefore does not appear to be a reasonable strain for assessing the toxicological effects of naxitamab.
L170: Y-mAbs did not conduct dedicated studies to assess fertility or carcinogenicity and these studies are not warranted to support the development of a drug intended to treat patients with advanced cancer. In addition, because the age of the proposed patient population is primarily \le 5 years old, FDA did not request an embryo-fetal development study to support the use of naxitamab. As IgG1 antibodies can cross the placenta with increasing frequency as pregnancy persists and because naxitamab can bind to normal GD2 expressing cells of neuroectodermal
L171: {DANYELZA, naxitamab-gqgk}
L172: origin, including the central nervous system and peripheral nerves, a warning for embryo-fetal toxicity is included in the label.
L173: Given the concerns regarding the model used for the toxicological assessment (nude rats), the nonclinical team cannot conclude that the toxicity of naxitamab has been adequately tested in animal models to look for any consequences related chronic administration of this product. Given the unusual development history, the application appears approvable based on the strength of the available clinical data in the proposed patient population with few clinical options; however, the nonclinical team will request a post-marketing requirement (PMR) to further explore the toxicity of naxitamab given for a minimum of 2 cycles in a relevant animal species. Because of the age of the intended patient population and the lack of significant clinical experience in adult patients and the focus on potential effects on nerve development, this PMR study should be conducted in juvenile animals.
L174: ## 5.2. Referenced NDAs, BLAs, DMFs
L175: ## The Applicant's Position:
L176: There are no referenced NDAs, BLAs, or DMFs related to nonclinical pharmacology or toxicology for naxitamab.
L177: ## The FDA's Assessment:
L178: There are no referenced NDAs, BLAs, or DMFs related to nonclinical pharmacology or toxicology for naxitamab.
L179: ## 5.3. Pharmacology
L180: ## Primary pharmacology (all data entered by FDA)
L181: ## A. In Vitro Studies
L182: ## Binding
L183: In Study #YMAB_GD2_AFFI_BIAC_ARP_02, naxitamab (hu3F8-IgG1) bound to human GD2 with greater affinity than either of the 14.18-based anti-GD2 antibodies, dinutuximab beta or Unituxin (K_D of 19.8 nM vs. 251 and 169 nM, respectively) as measured via surface plasmon resonance (SPR). In addition, the Applicant cited data from Cheung et al. 2012, showing that compared to alternate anti-GD2 antibodies 14.G2a and ME36.1, both humanized (hu3F8-IgG1 [naxitamab] and hu3F8-IgG4) and chimeric 3F8 antibodies had higher affinity for human GD2 as
L184: measured by SPR (Table 2). Naxitamab binding affinity for GD2 was similar to the affinity of the murine parent antibody, m3F8.
L185: **Table 5-1: Binding affinity of humanized and chimeric anti-GD2 antibodies for human GD2**
L186: Antibody	KD (nM)	Antibody	KD (nM)
hu3F8-IgG1	11	m3F8	5
hu3F8-IgG4	15	14.G2a	77
ch3F8-IgG1	13	ME36.1	19

L187: Hu – humanized antibodies. Ch – chimeric antibodies. m3F8 is a murine IgG3 anti-GD2 antibody. 14.G2a is derived from the clinically tested 14.18 anti-GD2 antibody (Cheung et al. 2012).
L188: Finally, naxitamab did not bind to gangliosides GM2, CD1a, GT1b, GD3, or GQ1b though there was minimal binding to GD1b (approximately 4.5% relative to GD2) as measured via ELISA (Cheung et al. 2012).
L189: ## *Antibody-mediated toxicity*
L190: Cheung et al. (2012) showed that in the absence of human serum or human effector cells naxitamab had cytotoxic activity against the GD2-expressing human neuroblastoma cell line, LAN-1, with an EC50 of 5 µg/mL, comparable or more potent compared to the previous clinical candidate m3F8 and a murine IgG2a isotype variant of another clinically studies anti-GD2 targeted antibody, 14.G2a (2 and 47 µg/mL, respectively). In addition, using a chromium release assay investigators compared naxitamab-induced antibody-dependent cellular cytotoxicity (ADCC) relative to parent antibody m3F8-induced ADCC using human peripheral blood mononuclear cells (PBMCs) in the presence of IL-2 or human polymorphonuclear leukocytes (PMN) as effector cells and LAN-1 cells as target cells at a 50:1 ratio in the presence of GM-CSF. Similar assays were conducted using IL-2 independent natural killer (NK-92MI) cells expressing human CD16 or CD32 Fc receptors at a ratio of 20:1 and without the addition of cytokines. In addition, the authors assessed GD2-directed antibody-mediated complement dependent cytotoxicity (CDC) compared to the murine parent antibody for naxitamab (m3F8). Relative to m3F8, naxitamab (hu3F8-IgG1) showed significant increases ADCC activity against the LAN-1 neuroblastoma cell in line in the PBMC and PMN assays as well as all other assays assessing ADCC (Table 3). Conversely, naxitamab had decreased CDC activity compared to the murine parent. The IgG4 isotype of hu3F8 did not have appreciable activity in any assay conducted (data not shown).

L213: Similarly, Munn and Cheung, 1990 showed that the m3F8 antibody was able to mediate antibody dependent cellular phagocytosis (ADCP). Investigators cultured human monocytes with CSF to enhance macrophage maturation and labeled cells with anti-CD14/CD-11b-PE antibodies then incubated these monocytes/macrophages with PKH2 (green fluorescent dye)-labelled GD2-expressing neuroblastoma cells (LAN-1) for 8 to 24 hours. The authors then measured internalization of green dye-labeled cells by microscopy or flow cytometry. In the presence of m3F8, there were high levels of target cell engulfment (data not shown). The authors did not include the clinical candidate, naxitamab, in this assessment.
L214: ## Species Selection
L215: To help justify use of the immune deficient nude rat as a relevant model for the toxicological assessment of naxitamab, in Study # NM52RT the Applicant evaluated the ability of naxitamab to mediate ADCC using effector cells from nude rats against rat GD2-expressing target cells and compared the results to naxitamab-induced ADCC using human effector cells against human GD2-expressing target cells.
L216: Evaluation of GD2 levels in GD2-expressing rat cell lines (B35 neuronal neuroblast, C6 glial tumor, F98 glioblastoma, PC12 adrenal pheochromocytoma) via flow cytometry led investigators to choose C6 and PC12 cells as rat target cells. Based on mean fluorescent intensity, naxitamab showed higher binding to C6 and PC12 cells compared to B35 and F98 rat cells but binding intensity was significantly lower on rat cells compared to human neuroblastoma LAN-1 cells known to express GD2 (Table 7). In addition, investigators assessed binding of naxitamab to natural killer (NK) cells from nude rats (identified using an anti-rat NK cell receptor CD161 antibody) in nude rat splenocytes. Double binding of naxitamab and CD161
L217: {DANYELZA, naxitamab-gqgk}
L218: occurred in approximately 19% of rat splenocytes, suggesting engagement of the human Fc domain of naxitamab by some percentage of NK cells in nude rats.

Triton (0.5%) served as a positive control for 100% cell death. Human LAN-1 cells incubated with rat splenocytes served as a positive control for activity of rat effector cells in the rat ADCC assays. In the presence of nude rat splenocytes naxitamab did not induce ADCC against rat PC12 cells but induced mild cytotoxicity in rat C6 cells (10% and 8% at 30 and 100 µg/mL, respectively), compared to absence of naxitamab (Table 8). L233: While the ADCC data does suggest that there was some ADCC activity by rat splenocytes, the activity using nude rat cells against rat targets was less robust than ADCC against human cells in the presence of human NK cells (maximum 10% for nude rat SC against rat GD-2 expressing cells vs. maximum 55% for human PBMCs against high expressing human GD-2 LAN-1 cells). This data did not provide clear support for the use of the nude rat as a species for toxicological assessment of naxitamab.
L234: ## B. In Vivo Studies
L235: Athymic mice implanted subcutaneously with LAN-1 tumors began twice weekly treatment with naxitamab at dose levels of 20 or 100 \mu\text{g} for a total of eight doses after tumors reached a volume of \sim 200 \text{ mm}^3. Treatment with naxitamab had minimal activity at the 20 \mu\text{g} dose level, with increased activity at 100 \mu\text{g}; m3F8 at 200\mu\text{g} on the same schedule had no clear effect (Cheung et al. 2012).
L236: **Figure 4: Anti-tumor activity of naxitamab and m3F8 in adult mice bearing subcutaneous LAN-1 tumors**
L237: (Excerpted from Cheung et al. 2012)
L238: Bergman and colleagues (1993) analyzed the activity of m3F8 in an epidural metastatic neuroblastoma model in nude rats using neurological outcome as measured by limb weakness as an endpoint. Paraparesis developed in 79% of rats 19 days post thoracic epidural implantation of human neuroblastoma cells (NMB7). Histological analysis of a single rat on Day 19 showed that the neuroblastoma tumors occupied approximately 50% of the epidural space; the remaining rats began treatment with m3F8 or ^{131}\text{I}-m3F8 at the initial sign of weakness with a 2nd dose 5-7 days later before necropsy 10 days post last treatment. Rats receiving no antibody or control antibodies (Table 10) became paraplegic and remained so until death, except for 2 (10%) rats with improvement from Grade 5 limb weakness to Grade 2/3 and no pathological evidence of tumors at necropsy despite evidence of myelopathy. Rats administered intravenous m3F8 (5 mg/kg) later showed improved neurological function (82% vs 10% controls), with initial improvement starting at 3.8 days and maximum improvement by 7.1 days. Six rats receiving m3F8 rats recovered normal neurological function which persisted until necropsy. In rats treated with radiolabeled ^{131}\text{I}-m3F8 (5 mCi/kg ^{131}\text{I} \sim3.125 mCi/mg m3F8), 100% of the treated rats showed improved neurological function from Grade 3.6 to 2.5 by Day 6.8, but 7 out of 9 rats (78%) deteriorated again prior to necropsy. Tumors contained a mean of 1.69% injected dose of ^{131}\text{I}-m3F8/g tumor weight (tumor weights ranged from 5 to 21 mg), and tumor to blood ratios varied from 0.7 to 16.2 in individual animals (mean = 4.46).

L242: {DANYELZA, naxitamab-gqgk}
L243: The Applicant submitted a series of publications using a surrogate anti-GD2 antibody targeting a separate GD2 epitope from naxitamab to explore naxitamab's known clinical side effect of pain. In Sorkin et al. 2002, (Sorkin LS, 2002) intravenous or intrathecal administration of an anti-GD2 monoclonal antibody (14G2a) to adult Sprague Dawley rats led to mechanical allodynia. Rats treated intravenously with 1 mg/kg of the antibody showed significant decrease in mechanical threshold for paw withdrawal in the 3 hours post administration (Figure 5). This allodynia was, however, limited to mechanical stimulation, as there was no effect on thermal withdrawal threshold following 1 or 3 mg/kg of intravenous anti-GD2 or 0.05 ng of intrathecal anti-GD2 (Figure 6). Control murine IgG2a had no effect on mechanical allodynia.

L259: ## Secondary Pharmacology
L260: ## The Applicant's Position:
L261: Y-mAbs has not conducted any studies dedicated to investigating secondary pharmacodynamics with naxitamab.
L262: ## The FDA's Assessment:
L263: Relevant pharmacology studies are discussed in the primary pharmacology section. Studies extensively investigating activity unrelated to the antibody target are not commonly expected for monoclonal antibodies.
L264: {DANYELZA, naxitamab-gqgk}
L265: ## Safety Pharmacology
L266: ## The Applicant's Position:
L267: In accordance with ICH S9 guidance, Nonclinical Evaluation for Anticancer Pharmaceuticals (October 2009), Y-mAbs has not conducted any standalone safety pharmacology studies with naxitamab.
L268: ## The FDA's Assessment:
L269: FDA agrees that stand-alone safety pharmacology studies are not expected for an antibody intended for the treatment of patients with advanced cancer. In general cardiovascular, CNS, and respiratory safety endpoints are included in general toxicity studies. See the general toxicity assessment for further details.

In Vivo Distribution of 131I-Naxitamab and 131I-mu3F8 in Nude Mice Harboring Human Neuroblastoma Xenografts[14]	Apart from the blood, normal tissues that were among the sites with the highest radioactivity distribution included the spleen (3.9% ID*/g), lung (3.5% ID/g), adrenal (3.1% ID/g), bladder (3.1% ID/g), liver (3.0% ID/g), and skin (4.0% ID/g). In tumor tissue, uptake was 29.6% ID/g after 131I naxitamab administration.
These results demonstrate that naxitamab has the desired targeting efficiency for in vivo use as an antitumor immunotherapeutic.

Similar results were observed with four cycles of dosing (during Weeks 1, 5, 9, and 13), with a nonlinear, dose-dependent increase in naxitamab exposure over the dose range tested, which resulted in reduced exposure in general in females compared with males, and moderate systemic accumulation in both sexes after four cycles of treatment

L277: ## The Applicant's Position:
L278: The nonclinical pharmacokinetic studies were designed to characterize the absorption and clearance of naxitamab after either single or repeated dosing by intravenous injection in mice and rats and to characterize distribution of naxitamab at target and off-target sites by evaluating immunoreactivity and biodistribution of naxitamab using in vitro and in vivo test methods.
L279: Single-dose pharmacokinetic studies evaluating the absorption and blood clearance of naxitamab were conducted in mice. Total systemic clearance was measured after a single intravenous injection of 131I naxitamab in C57BL/6 and BALB/c athymic nude mice. Systemic clearance of 131I naxitamab was substantially faster in nude mice when compared with that in C57BL/6 mice. In a second study, serum pharmacokinetics of naxitamab were investigated after an intravenous injection in nude mice. In this study, the serum terminal half-life of naxitamab was calculated to be 17 hours.
L280: In repeat-dose toxicity studies in nude rats, serum pharmacokinetics were measured after the final intravenous dose of naxitamab in each of Cycles 1 and 2 of a 6-week study or Cycles 1 and 4 of a 13-week study in athymic Hsd:RH-Foxn1nu nude rats. In both studies, the extent of exposure after naxitamab administration was characterized by nonlinear, dose-dependent kinetics over the dose range tested; additionally, after either two or four cycles of treatment with naxitamab, there was evidence of accumulation in the latter dosing cycle. In general, exposure in females was slightly lower than in males.
L281: {DANYELZA, naxitamab-gqgk}
L282: The potential distribution and affinity of naxitamab to organs of interest were evaluated by in vitro and in vivo test methods. In an in vitro tissue immunoreactivity study, the relative affinity of naxitamab was assessed in selected frozen human tissue sections (normal organ and muscle tissue, and neuroblastoma tissues) to better characterize the potential distribution and reactivity of the antibody across tissues of interest. In general, aside from tumor tissues, naxitamab showed relatively strong affinity to the brain, cerebellum, and spinal cord from healthy tissue sections and weak affinity to the lung, sigmoid colon, stomach, and testes. In an in vivo distribution study, a single intravenous dose of ^{131}\text{I} naxitamab was administered to athymic nude mice harboring xenografted subcutaneous human neuroblastoma tumors. The mouse model used in the biodistribution study, athymic nude mice harboring subcutaneous human neuroblastoma xenografts, was the same model used for in vivo primary pharmacodynamics studies evaluating the efficacy of naxitamab in an animal model of disease. In distribution studies in mice, naxitamab preferentially accumulated in tumor tissue compared with nontumor tissues.
L283: ## **The FDA's Assessment:**
L284: FDA agrees with the Applicant's analysis of distribution of intravenous ^{131}\text{I}-hu3F8-IgG1 (naxitamab) and ^{131}\text{I}-m3F8 (100 \mu\text{Ci}) in adult female athymic nude mice bearing subcutaneous LAN-1 neuroblastoma tumors showing accumulation mostly in tumors (29% of injected dose/gram of mouse), followed by blood, spleen, liver, adrenal, bladder, lung, and skin (2.1-4.2%), though use of a high GD-2 expressing implanted human tumor may exaggerate the distribution of the antibody to tumor vs normal tissue.
L285: FDA could not confirm the difference in absorption and clearance of naxitamab in the C5BL6 vs. nude mouse; however, this data was not critical for the nonclinical safety assessment of this product. Studies investigating metabolism and excretion are not commonly expected for monoclonal antibodies.
L286: FDA agrees that tissue cross reactivity studies showed binding to peripheral nerve, cerebellum, spinal cord, frontal lobe, and pons. Basal epithelium of the skin was another important target in some studies. In additional studies to those discussed above, the Applicant determined the tissue cross-reactivity of naxitamab (hu3F8) in Study #PK86DG and compared the cross-reactivity of naxitamab (hu3F8) and Unituxin (an alternate anti-GD2 antibody targeting a different epitope) in Study #DL25SP using frozen tissues from humans (3 donors), Sprague-Dawley (SD) rats, and Hsd:RH *Foxn1mu* (nude) rats. Tissues were limited to skin containing peripheral nerve, tibial peripheral nerve, cerebellum, spinal cord, frontal lobe, and pons from each species. Positive staining occurred in cerebral cortex, cerebellum, spinal cord, and
L287: peripheral nerve tissues for naxitamab from each species, representing on target binding. There were no significant differences in intensity or distribution of the staining between the two antibodies in human tissues. Naxitamab staining in human tissues had increased intensity compared to tissues from either rat strain in both studies. Additional naxitamab staining included basal epithelium of the skin in human tissues and follicular epithelium of the skin in both rat species in both studies. Non-specific staining with naxitamab and Unituxin occurred at levels similar to those that observed with isotype controls in the white matter from neural tissues analyzed from human donors. No non-specific staining occurred in either rat species using naxitamab.

L290: ## Data (presented by Applicant):
L291: ## **Study title/ Study number:**
L292: A 13-Week Toxicity Study by Intravenous Infusion Administration to Hsd:RH-Foxn1rn Nude Rats Followed by a 4-Week Recovery Period (YN70HM)
L293: ## **Key Study Findings**
L294: - The intravenous infusion administration of naxitamab (an anti-GD2 mAb) to Hsd:RH-Foxn1rn nude rats, over four cycles of 3 treatment days, up to 50 mg/kg per occasion was generally well tolerated over the 13-week dosing period. Body weight gain was slightly affected with treatment; however, no other treatment-related effects were noted with respect to clinical evaluations (including general health and injection-site examinations), food consumption, nociception response, or ophthalmoscopic examination.
- Treatment-related findings on organ weights and on hematology and blood chemistry parameters were considered nonadverse. In general, these effects were mild, lacking dose-related trends, and reversible.
- Adverse findings in histopathology evaluations of the stomach, including hyperplasia with inflammation in the glandular mucosa of the stomach in males at 50 mg/kg per occasion and marked erosions of the glandular mucosa of the stomach in females at greater than or equal to 10 mg/kg per occasion, resulted in the NOAEL to be established at 10 mg/kg/day (30 mg/kg per cycle) in male rats only, corresponding to a Cmax of 0.282 mg/mL and AUC0-216 h of 19.9 mg h/mL
L295: {DANYELZA, naxitamab-gqgk}
L296: after four dosing cycles.
L297: **FDA Additional Comment on Key Findings:** In addition to the findings listed above, histopathology findings of inflammatory cell infiltrates and inflammation occurred in the plantar and tibial nerves.

L304: ## **Observations and Results: changes from control**
L305: Parameters	Major Findings
Mortality	No treatment-related mortality occurred. There were 7 unscheduled deaths during the study, including 2 control animals, 3 animals that were treated at 10 mg/kg/day, and 2 animals that were treated at 50 mg/kg/day. Of the deaths, 5 occurred during the treatment phase of the study, and 2 occurred during the recovery phase. All were considered incidental and not test article-related.
Clinical Signs	There were no other clinical signs of note, including any from general health examinations or injection site observations, that were considered to be outside of normal limits.
Body Weights	Compared with controls, there was an apparent trend of slower rates of weight gain in naxitamab-treated males at both dose levels (88%-94% of control animal's weight) and in females treated at both dose levels (89%-91% of control animal's weight) from Days 1 to 95 of the study. In the recovery phase, mean weight value gains were also reduced in males treated at either dose level. On the basis of these trends, body weight was considered to be affected by naxitamab treatment.
Ophthalmoscopy	There were no treatment-related ophthalmoscopic findings.
Hematology	Nonadverse, reversible effects on hematology parameters were observed with treatment, including increased reticulocyte counts in females at Week 6 (1.11x) and increased total leukocyte counts in males at both dose levels at Week 6 (1.11-1.32x) and at Week 13 (1.33-1.43x). Dose-related increases in neutrophils, lymphocytes, eosinophils, and basophils were observed in males at Week 6 (1.10-1.73x) and, except for basophils, also at Week 13 (1.16-1.61x). At Week 13, monocytes were also elevated in naxitamab-treated males (1.70-1.87x). Shorter prothrombin times were noted in both sexes and with either dose level at Week 6 (0.96-0.82x) and Week 13 (0.91-0.80x). The hematological effects recovered by the end of the 4-week recovery period after the last cycle of dosing.
Clinical Chemistry	Nonadverse effects on blood chemistry parameters were observed after either two or four dosing cycles. At Week 13, the changes included increased urea concentrations in both sexes at the high dose level (1.12-1.15x) and increased glucose concentrations in males at either dose level (1.16-1.23x). Except for glucose levels in males treated at the high dose of naxitamab, all changes in blood chemistry recovered by the end of the recovery period. The treatment-related
L306: {DANYELZA, naxitamab-gqgk}
L307: effects were generally mild, not dose related, and not associated with macroscopic or histologic organ findings.

FDA Additional Comment: There was a 13% increase in Potassium in HD males at the 6 week timepoint.
Urinalysis	The composition of urine was unaffected by treatment.
Gross Pathology	The macroscopic examination performed in animals after 4 weeks of recovery revealed changes in the stomach. Internal depressions were observed in one female previously treated with 50 mg/kg per occasion, two females previously treated with 10 mg/kg per occasion, and one control female. Thickening of the stomach wall was also observed in one female, which was previously treated with 10 mg/kg per occasion.
Organ Weights	Nonadverse, reversible changes in organ weights were observed, consisting of higher mean adjusted liver weights in males at the high dose level (1.10x), lower mean adjusted thyroid and parathyroid weights at either dose level in both sexes (0.67-0.74x in males, 0.88x in females), and higher spleen weights in females treated at the high dose level (1.16x). Minor changes in reproductive organ weights were observed in both sexes at both dose levels, including increased prostate weights (1.08-1.11x) and decreased seminal vesicle weights (0.89-0.97x) in males and elevated uterus and cervix weights (1.08-1.11x) in females. In general, these findings were not statistically significant and lacked a dose-related trend. Except for the findings in reproductive organs in animals treated at the high dose level, all changes in organ weights recovered 4 weeks after dosing. The persistent, minor changes in reproductive organ weights were of unknown etiology, but they were of small magnitude and were not associated with histological findings, suggesting that the changes were nonadverse.
Histopathology	Treatment-related histopathology changes were confined to inflammatory and/or hyperplastic changes of the glandular mucosa of stomachs in males treated with 50 mg/kg per occasion, and erosions of the glandular mucosa of the stomach in females treated with 10 mg/kg per occasion and in both sexes treated with 50 mg/kg per occasion. In the stomach, erosion of the glandular mucosa mostly correlated with macroscopic depressions of the glandular stomach, and hyperplasia of the glandular mucosa mostly correlated with macroscopic thickening of the mucosa. Erosions of the glandular mucosa were sometimes accompanied by inflammation and/or edema, mostly in the males. After the 4-week off-dose period, there was a full recovery of both the macroscopic and microscopic findings
L308: {DANYELZA, naxitamab-gqgk}
L309: seen in the stomach in males. In females, there was full recovery of the hyperplasia, inflammation, and edema of the glandular mucosa, and partial recovery of the glandular mucosa erosions.
L310: **FDA Additional Comment on Histopathology (table added by FDA):** In addition to the histopathological findings described above, there were occasional findings of inflammation in the nerves in males.

L312: Other Evaluations	Assessment of pain (nociception) response was performed in Study YN70HM. There was no clear effect of treatment on pain threshold on Days 1, 29, and 85 in the animals because the response in the treated animals showed no significant magnitude of change from pretreatment and/or concurrent controls. A statistically significant difference of note was the increased mean values immediately after dosing in Week 1 compared with predose in the treated females. However, because there was considerable overlap of individual values across the groups and no effect in females in Weeks 5 and 13, and no effect in males in Weeks 1, 5, and 13, this finding could not be clearly attributed to treatment.

L316: ## The Applicant's Position:
L317: The Hsd:RH-*Foxn1*tmu nude rat species has been used for the pivotal toxicity studies and toxicokinetic evaluations to mitigate the potential risk of a strong antidrug antibody response, which would limit the value of longer-term chronic toxicity studies in outbred laboratory rat strains (e.g., Sprague-Dawley or Wistar rats). Y-mAbs evaluated on-target tissue reactivity in two GLP-compliant studies by using selected tissues of human and rat origin to support the species selected for toxicology studies (Studies PK86GD and DL25SP). A non-GLP in vitro study comparing the ability of naxitamab to mediate an immune response by cells from nude rats against rat GD2-expressing cells versus human cells against human GD2-expressing cells showed that naxitamab induced ADCC in human and nude rat systems. The binding profile comparisons and the functional assays conducted with cells from the nude rat species, in addition to other factors that substantiate the use of the nude rat species in toxicology studies (e.g., its historical use in regulatory toxicology studies and its retention of intact relevant downstream systems, such as natural killer cells, macrophages, and the complement system), justify the selection of the Hsd: RH-*Foxn1*tmu nude rat species for pivotal toxicity studies and toxicokinetic evaluations.
L318: The safety profile of naxitamab is based on a single-dose toxicity study in mice and repeat-dose toxicology studies (6- and 13-week) in Hsd: RH-*Foxn1*tmu nude rats. In brief, for the repeat-dose studies, in the 6-week study, nonadverse changes in hematological and biochemical parameters and organ weights were observed and considered to be treatment-related; however, these changes were generally minor and were not correlated with macroscopic or microscopic findings. Because all findings were considered incidental or nonadverse in this study, the NOAEL was determined to be 50 mg/kg/day (150 mg/kg per cycle) for both male and female rats, corresponding to a mean (sexes combined) Cmax of 1.57 mg/mL and AUC0-216 h of 86.15 mg·h/mL after two dosing cycles. In the 13-week study, adverse findings in histopathology evaluations of the stomach resulted in the NOAEL to be established at 10 mg/kg/day (30 mg/kg per cycle) in male rats only, corresponding to a Cmax of 0.282 mg/mL and AUC0-216 h of 19.9 mg·h/mL after four dosing cycles.
L319: In mice and rats, it was demonstrated that naxitamab had acceptable safety margin estimates based on body weight scaling of the NOAEL to the human equivalent dose. Overall, Y-mAbs asserts that the safety profile of naxitamab established in this focused nonclinical program, particularly in the context of the extensive clinical history of use of naxitamab, murine 3F8, and other anti-GD2 antibodies that demonstrates an adequate safety profile, supports the use of naxitamab for the treatment of neuroblastoma.
L320: {DANYELZA, naxitamab-gqgk}
L321: ## **The FDA's Assessment:**
L322: While FDA generally agrees with the Applicant's description of the findings in the nude rat toxicology study, the Agency does not agree that the nude rat adequately recapitulates the critical toxicological attributes of the product to fully inform on the safety of naxitamab. Naxitamab-mediated toxicities in the nude rat in the 13-week study were generally minor with mild GI toxicity (decreased weight gain, minimal to moderate histopathological erosion in the glandular stomach), decreased thyroid weight, and an increased incidence of inflammation of nerves (male rats only). Similar clinical signs and pathology occurred in a 6-week intravenous GLP-compliant study in nude rats conducted by the Sponsor in which naxitamab was administered at 0, 10, or 50 mg/kg for 2 cycles (Week 1; Days 1, 3, 5 and Week 5; Days 29, 31, 33) including decreased weight gain, hematology and clinical chemistry changes, and histopathology. Evaluation of nociception via foot pinch indicated no change in pain threshold following mechanical stimulation with a blunt pointer at any dose level, as observed in the 13-week study.
L323: While nude mice and rats are common strains employed for pharmacological activity assessment of drugs, particularly in tumor implantation models, they are not typically used as the primary model for the toxicological safety assessment of either drugs or biologics. The Applicant explained that the NK cells, neutrophils, and macrophages that drive the killing of naxitamab-bound cells are present in nude rats and that, therefore, the use of this strain would permit for a reasonable safety assessment in rodents even in chronic toxicity studies as the animals would not develop anti-product antibodies that would limit exposure to the test-article. FDA acknowledged both that these cells were present and the Applicant's goal of circumventing the development of anti-drug antibodies, but expressed some reservations regarding the use of an immune deficient model to model the safety of a product with a mechanism of action based on immune-mediated cell killing and suggested that the Applicant conduct an in vitro study comparing the ADCC activity of naxitamab in a nude rat system vs. a human system at several points during the clinical development of the product to help support the use of this model. The Applicant has provided this data with the BLA submission; however, the data shows minimal activity using nude rat cells vs. human cells as effector cells. This data, in addition to the lack of effects on nociception demonstrated in the nude rat, suggests that this strain does not adequately recapitulate the toxicity of naxitamab. Literature cited by the Applicant using other antibodies targeting GD2 in fully immunocompetent rat or mouse models demonstrates the ability of these rodents to model the allodynia observed clinically, further suggesting that though some immune-competent rodent models may adequately predict the toxicity of the antibody the nude rat is does not, making it an inadequate toxicology model for this product.
L324: {DANYELZA, naxitamab-gqgk}

L327: **Study title/Study number:** Naxitamab: Preliminary Juvenile Toxicity Study in the Hsd:RH-Foxn1ru Nude Rat by Intraperitoneal Injection and Intravenous Infusion Administration (CB61CK)
L328: ## Key Study Findings
L329: - Treatment with naxitamab at dose levels up to and including 50 mg/kg/occasion was well tolerated. No premature deaths occurred during the course of the study, and there were no changes in general clinical condition or postdosing signs that were related to naxitamab administration.
- There were no macroscopic abnormalities that were clearly attributable to naxitamab administration and no histopathological changes were apparent in the stomach of animals given 50 mg/kg per occasion.
- On the basis of the results obtained in this study, it was concluded that the dose level of 50 mg/kg per occasion (limited by the maximum volume/infusion rate in the required dose duration) would be suitable for use as the high dose level in a main juvenile toxicity study, if deemed applicable.

L350: ## **Observations and Results: changes from control**
L351: Parameters	Major Findings
Mortality	No premature deaths occurred during the course of the study.
Clinical Signs	There were no changes in general clinical condition or postdosing signs that were related to naxitamab administration.
Body Weights	Group mean body weight gain for females given naxitamab at 50 mg/kg per occasion during the two dosing cycles was slightly lower than control animals, with statistical significance attained for the differences during both dosing cycles. The body weight gain of males given 50 mg/kg per occasion was slightly lower than control animals during the intraperitoneal dosing cycle, but statistical significance was not attained. Overall mean body weight gain in both sexes in this dose group during PND 12-54 was unaffected.
The body weight performance of males and females given 5/7.5/10 or 30 mg/kg per occasion was unaffected.
Gross Pathology	At scheduled termination on PND 54, one male in the 30-mg/kg per occasion group and one female in the 5-/7.5-/10-mg/kg per occasion group had diffuse dark areas in the glandular mucosa of the stomach. In addition, one male in the 50-mg/kg per occasion group and one female in the 5-/7.5-/10-mg/kg per occasion group had a liver mass.
Histopathology	Microscopic examination of the stomach of animals in the control and 50-mg/kg per occasion groups revealed no test item-related findings. The incidence and distribution of all findings were considered to be unrelated to treatment.
Other Evaluations	The pain threshold of males was not clearly affected by naxitamab, although it was noted during the predose assessment that the pain threshold of males in the 50-mg/kg per occasion group was reached at a slightly lower force (50 g) than in controls (59 g).
In females, while there was no effect on pain threshold during the predose assessment, following dose administration there was a clear and dose-dependent decrease in pain threshold in the 30- or 50-mg/kg per occasion groups.
L352: {DANYELZA, naxitamab-gqgk}
L353: There was no clear evidence of an effect of naxitamab administration on the age or body weight of attainment of sexual maturation.
L354: ## The Applicant's Position:
L355: This preliminary study assessed the influence of naxitamab, when administered to juvenile Hsd:RH-Foxn1nu nude rats at doses up to 50 mg/kg per occasion from PND 12 over two cycles of 3 treatment days: on Days 1, 3, and 5 (PND 12, 14, and 16) by intraperitoneal injection, and on Days 29, 31, and 33 (PND 40, 42, and 44) by intravenous infusion for up to 60 minutes.
L356: Based on the results obtained in this study, it was concluded that the dose level of 50 mg/kg per occasion (limited by the maximum volume/infusion rate in the required dose duration) would be suitable for use as the high dose level in a main juvenile toxicity study, if deemed applicable.
L357: ## The FDA's Assessment:
L358: FDA did not review this study in detail because it was a dose range finding study using a limited number of animals in a strain that does not appear to be a model that adequately recapitulates the toxicity of naxitamab and did not include the primary histopathology data. No deaths or clinical observations occurred in this study, with the exception of decreased body weight gain in the high dose group. No changes in pain thresholds were present in males and effects in females were too highly variable to make any conclusions.

L370: ## 5.5.4. Reproductive and Developmental Toxicology
L371: ## The Applicant's Position:
L372: Considering the proposed patient population, it is unlikely that reproductive and developmental studies in animals will contribute meaningful safety data relevant to the indicated population; therefore, Y-mAbs has not conducted reproductive toxicology studies with naxitamab.
L373: ## The FDA's Assessment:
L374: No reproductive and developmental toxicology studies were conducted to support the submission of the BLA for naxitamab. Based on the age of the proposed patient population, FDA agreed that traditional reproductive toxicology studies were not warranted to support the use of this product in the treatment of patients with maintenance phase of high-risk neuroblastoma, as based on a review by Brodeur and Maris 2003, neuroblastoma is defined as a pediatric cancer, with the average age of diagnosis being 18 months with about 40% of patients diagnosed by 1 year of age, 75% by 4 years and 98% by 10 years of age.
L375: {DANYELZA, naxitamab-gqgk}
